- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### MemorySim: An RTL-level, timing accurate simulator model for the Chisel ecosystem
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12636v1)
- **Authors**: Ansh Chaurasia
- **Abstract**: The rapid growth of AI applications has driven increased demand for specialized AI hardware, highlighting critical opportunities within the memory subsystem, which often serves as a performance bottleneck in high-demand workloads such as large language models (LLMs). Existing high-level memory simulators, such as DRAMSim2 and DRAMSim3, offer timing simulations but frequently compromise on correctness or integration at the register-transfer level (RTL). We present MemorySim, an RTL-level memory simulator designed to deliver both accurate timing and functional correctness. MemorySim integrates seamlessly with existing Chisel and Verilog simulations and is fully compatible with the Chisel/Chipyard ecosystem. This enables users to obtain precise performance and power estimates, supporting downstream evaluation through simulation platforms such as FireSim.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MemorySim paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MemorySim, an RTL-level memory simulator designed for the Chisel/Chipyard ecosystem. It addresses the limitations of existing high-level DRAM models like DRAMSim2/3, which lack the timing fidelity, resource constraints, and power-performance coupling needed for accurate AI accelerator memory subsystem analysis. MemorySim aims to provide accurate timing simulations while maintaining data correctness through cycle-accurate handshake protocols. The simulator is built with modular components (memory controller, bank scheduler, DRAM timing model) and supports experimentation through standalone trace-based simulations and integration with hardware generators and FPGA-accelerated emulation flows. The paper evaluates MemorySim by comparing its performance against DRAMSim3 using microbenchmarks, highlighting the cycle overheads and sensitivities to parameters like queue size.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in providing an RTL-level memory simulator tightly integrated into the Chisel/Chipyard ecosystem, offering a more detailed and potentially accurate simulation compared to higher-level models. While existing tools like Ramulator and USIMM offer cycle-accurate simulations, they often involve complex C++ interfaces and are difficult to integrate within an RTL-centric workflow. MemorySim avoids these issues by being written in Chisel and directly leveraging the Chipyard ecosystem.
*   **Significance:** Accurate memory simulation is crucial for designing efficient AI accelerators. The paper argues that existing high-level models are insufficient for capturing the intricacies of memory behavior under AI workloads. If MemorySim can deliver on its promise of higher accuracy and easier integration, it could significantly impact the design process for memory subsystems within AI chips. The direct integration with FireSim is also significant as it allows for FPGA-accelerated simulation and verification.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **RTL-Level Accuracy:** Addresses limitations of higher-level simulators by capturing finer-grained timing and resource constraints.
    *   **Ecosystem Integration:** Leverages Chisel/Chipyard, simplifying integration and co-simulation with existing designs.
    *   **Modular Architecture:** Allows for flexible configuration and experimentation.
    *   **FPGA Acceleration Support:** Enables faster simulation through FireSim.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation primarily focuses on comparing MemorySim against DRAMSim3. While this provides insight into the cycle overheads and parameter sensitivities, a more thorough evaluation would include comparisons with real hardware measurements or other existing RTL-level memory models (if any are directly comparable in terms of integration).
    *   **Performance Overhead:** The results show a significant cycle overhead compared to DRAMSim3, which could limit the scalability of simulations, especially for large AI workloads.
    *   **Feature Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations like the lack of caching and request re-ordering in the current implementation.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Analysis with other RTL-based memory simulators:** This is a critical oversight. The paper needs to position itself against alternatives, even if they aren't directly integrated with Chisel. What are the advantages and disadvantages of MemorySim compared to other existing RTL-based solutions for memory modeling?
*   **Potential Impact:** If the performance overhead can be mitigated and the feature set expanded, MemorySim has the potential to become a valuable tool for AI accelerator designers. The tight integration with Chipyard and FireSim provides a strong foundation for adoption.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of memory simulation by providing an RTL-level simulator integrated with Chisel/Chipyard. However, the evaluation is limited, and the performance overhead and feature limitations need to be addressed. The lack of comparison to alternative RTL solutions significantly impacts the novelty. While the potential impact is significant, it hinges on overcoming these limitations. Therefore, a score of 6 seems appropriate. The simulator has potential and is a step in the right direction, but needs significantly more work and rigorous evaluation before it can be considered a truly impactful tool.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Diagnostic-Guided Dynamic Profile Optimization for LLM-based User Simulators in Sequential Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12645v1)
- **Authors**: Hongyang Liu, Zhu Sun, Tianjun Wei, Yan Wang, Jiajie Zhu, Xinghua Qu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled realistic user simulators for developing and evaluating recommender systems (RSs). However, existing LLM-based simulators for RSs face two major limitations: (1) static and single-step prompt-based inference that leads to inaccurate and incomplete user profile construction; (2) unrealistic and single-round recommendation-feedback interaction pattern that fails to capture real-world scenarios. To address these limitations, we propose DGDPO (Diagnostic-Guided Dynamic Profile Optimization), a novel framework that constructs user profile through a dynamic and iterative optimization process to enhance the simulation fidelity. Specifically, DGDPO incorporates two core modules within each optimization loop: firstly, a specialized LLM-based diagnostic module, calibrated through our novel training strategy, accurately identifies specific defects in the user profile. Subsequently, a generalized LLM-based treatment module analyzes the diagnosed defect and generates targeted suggestions to refine the profile. Furthermore, unlike existing LLM-based user simulators that are limited to single-round interactions, we are the first to integrate DGDPO with sequential recommenders, enabling a bidirectional evolution where user profiles and recommendation strategies adapt to each other over multi-round interactions. Extensive experiments conducted on three real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Diagnostic-Guided Dynamic Profile Optimization for LLM-based User Simulators in Sequential Recommendation" addresses two key limitations of existing LLM-based user simulators used in recommender systems: (1) inaccurate/incomplete user profiles due to static prompt-based inference, and (2) unrealistic single-round interactions.  The authors propose a novel framework called Diagnostic-Guided Dynamic Profile Optimization (DGDPO). DGDPO dynamically refines user profiles through iterative optimization. This involves a specialized LLM-based diagnostic module for identifying profile defects and a generalized LLM-based treatment module for suggesting refinements. The framework is integrated with sequential recommenders to enable a bidirectional evolution of user profiles and recommendation strategies over multi-round interactions. Experimental results on three real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of DGDPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects. First, the concept of dynamically optimizing user profiles in LLM-based simulators is a valuable contribution.  While self-reflection approaches exist, the authors introduce a specialized diagnostic module, addressing hallucination issues and improving accuracy in identifying profile defects. Second, the integration of the simulator with sequential recommenders is a crucial step towards more realistic multi-round interaction simulations, filling a gap in the current literature. The domain-adaptive pre-training and defect-specific fine-tuning strategy for the diagnostic LLM contribute to the technical novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a fundamental problem in recommender system evaluation: the limitations of existing user simulators.  By providing a more realistic and controllable simulation environment, DGDPO can significantly improve the development and evaluation of new recommendation algorithms, ultimately leading to better user experiences. The impact on the research community could be significant, as DGDPO provides a practical and effective tool for evaluating sequential recommenders and understanding the interplay between user profiles and recommendation strategies over time.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LLM-based user simulators.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** DGDPO is well-defined and logically structured, with clear explanations of its core components.
    *   **Technical Rigor:** The paper demonstrates a solid technical approach, including specialized LLM training strategies and integration with sequential recommenders.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, with comparisons against relevant baselines on multiple real-world datasets. Ablation studies are also performed to show the importance of the components.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide detailed implementation details, enhancing the potential for reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not explicitly address the computational cost of the approach, particularly the reliance on multiple LLM calls in each iteration. This could be a significant bottleneck for large-scale simulations.
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Diagnostic Module:** While the specialized LLM-based diagnostic module shows improvement, the specifics of defect identification are likely domain-dependent. Further research is needed to explore how well this component generalizes across different types of data and user behaviors.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Dependency:** The success of both the diagnostic and treatment modules relies heavily on prompt engineering. While the paper details the prompts used, the sensitivity of performance to prompt variations is not thoroughly investigated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The proposed method enables more accurate and realistic simulations, facilitating the design and evaluation of more effective sequential recommendation algorithms. The dynamic user profiles are of interest to the research community.
*   **Justification of Score:** The score reflects the paper's significant novelty in the LLM user simulation domain and the practical value of the proposed framework. The weaknesses, primarily related to computational costs and generalizability of the diagnostic module, prevent the paper from receiving an even higher score.

### Score-informed Neural Operator for Enhancing Ordering-based Causal Discovery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12650v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyeon Kang, Songseong Kim, Chanhui Lee, Doyeong Hwang, Joanie Hayoun Chung, Yunkyung Ko, Sumin Lee, Sungwoong Kim, Sungbin Lim
- **Abstract**: Ordering-based approaches to causal discovery identify topological orders of causal graphs, providing scalable alternatives to combinatorial search methods. Under the Additive Noise Model (ANM) assumption, recent causal ordering methods based on score matching require an accurate estimation of the Hessian diagonal of the log-densities. However, previous approaches mainly use Stein gradient estimators, which are computationally expensive and memory-intensive. Although DiffAN addresses these limitations by substituting kernel-based estimates with diffusion models, it remains numerically unstable due to the second-order derivatives of score models. To alleviate these problems, we propose Score-informed Neural Operator (SciNO), a probabilistic generative model in smooth function spaces designed to stably approximate the Hessian diagonal and to preserve structural information during the score modeling. Empirical results show that SciNO reduces order divergence by 42.7% on synthetic graphs and by 31.5% on real-world datasets on average compared to DiffAN, while maintaining memory efficiency and scalability. Furthermore, we propose a probabilistic control algorithm for causal reasoning with autoregressive models that integrates SciNO's probability estimates with autoregressive model priors, enabling reliable data-driven causal ordering informed by semantic information. Consequently, the proposed method enhances causal reasoning abilities of LLMs without additional fine-tuning or prompt engineering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Score-informed Neural Operator (SciNO), a novel functional diffusion model for enhancing ordering-based causal discovery under the Additive Noise Model (ANM) assumption. SciNO addresses limitations of previous methods like SCORE, CaPS, and DiffAN, which rely on accurate estimation of the Hessian diagonal of log-densities. SciNO leverages neural operators in a Hilbert space to stably approximate the Hessian diagonal, preserving structural information during score modeling. The paper also proposes a probabilistic control algorithm that integrates SciNO's probability estimates with autoregressive model priors for causal reasoning, potentially enhancing LLM's causal reasoning abilities without further fine-tuning.  Empirical results demonstrate SciNO's improved order divergence compared to DiffAN on synthetic and real-world datasets, while maintaining memory efficiency and scalability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in two key areas: 1) the SciNO architecture itself, which is a functional diffusion model using neural operators designed for stable Hessian approximation, and 2) the probabilistic control algorithm for enhancing causal reasoning in LLMs using SciNO's estimates.  While the use of neural operators for function approximation is not entirely novel, the specific application to Hessian diagonal estimation for causal discovery, with modifications to the architecture tailored to this purpose (LTE module and complex spectral decomposition), does represent a significant advancement. The integration with LLMs for causal reasoning is also a novel approach that leverages score-informed statistics.
* **Significance:**  The significance is primarily in addressing the limitations of existing score-matching-based causal discovery methods, namely the computational cost and numerical instability associated with derivative estimation. SciNO offers a more scalable and stable alternative, which is particularly relevant for high-dimensional datasets. The LLM integration has the potential to improve the causal reasoning capabilities of these models, a topic of increasing importance.  The fact that the improvements are achieved without costly fine-tuning or prompt engineering also makes it attractive.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides substantial empirical evidence, demonstrating significant improvements in order divergence on both synthetic and real-world datasets compared to DiffAN. The memory efficiency improvements are also well-documented.
    * **Principled Approach:** The use of functional diffusion models and neural operators is grounded in theoretical considerations, providing a more stable and reliable approach to Hessian estimation.
    * **Practical Application:** The application to causal reasoning with LLMs is timely and addresses a relevant problem, with potentially broad applicability.
    * **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow, clearly outlining the proposed method and its advantages.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides a theoretical justification for SciNO, a more in-depth analysis of its convergence properties and generalization bounds would strengthen the contribution. The proofs of the theorems are somewhat sketchy and require more rigorous elaboration.
    * **Dependency on ANM:** The method is still dependent on the Additive Noise Model (ANM) assumption, which limits its applicability in some real-world scenarios. A discussion of the limitations imposed by the ANM assumption and potential extensions to other causal models would be beneficial.
    * **LLM Control Experiment Detail:** While demonstrating improvements in LLM causal reasoning, the specific prompting strategy and LLM setup could benefit from more detailed discussion, particularly surrounding the sensitivity of the results to different LLM configurations and datasets with varying levels of descriptions.
    * **Scalability for LLM Usage:** The experiments using LLMs are promising, but the cost associated with applying SciNO and querying an LLM, especially for complex, high-dimensional causal problems, is an element that is not emphasized, and this needs to be highlighted.

**Overall Justification:**

Despite the weaknesses identified, SciNO presents a valuable contribution to the field of causal discovery. It addresses a crucial bottleneck in score-matching-based methods and provides a practical approach for improving the causal reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods. The theoretical grounding in functional diffusion models provides a solid foundation for future research. However, more rigorous theoretical analysis is crucial for the paper to have a larger impact and to justify some of the algorithmic choices. While the paper offers a solid and well-justified advance, the dependence of the results on the LLM architecture requires more experiments on multiple LLMs to improve the generalization power of this framework.

### Leveraging Large Language Models for Predictive Analysis of Human Misery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12669v1)
- **Authors**: Bishanka Seal, Rahul Seetharaman, Aman Bansal, Abhilash Nandy
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for predicting human-perceived misery scores from natural language descriptions of real-world scenarios. The task is framed as a regression problem, where the model assigns a scalar value from 0 to 100 to each input statement. We evaluate multiple prompting strategies, including zero-shot, fixed-context few-shot, and retrieval-based prompting using BERT sentence embeddings. Few-shot approaches consistently outperform zero-shot baselines, underscoring the value of contextual examples in affective prediction. To move beyond static evaluation, we introduce the "Misery Game Show", a novel gamified framework inspired by a television format. It tests LLMs through structured rounds involving ordinal comparison, binary classification, scalar estimation, and feedback-driven reasoning. This setup enables us to assess not only predictive accuracy but also the model's ability to adapt based on corrective feedback. The gamified evaluation highlights the broader potential of LLMs in dynamic emotional reasoning tasks beyond standard regression. Code and data link: https://github.com/abhi1nandy2/Misery_Data_Exps_GitHub
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for predicting human-perceived misery scores from text descriptions of real-world scenarios. The study frames this as a regression problem, where an LLM assigns a score from 0 to 100 to each input statement.  It investigates different prompting strategies: zero-shot, few-shot, and retrieval-augmented prompting. The authors introduce a novel "Misery Game Show" framework to evaluate LLMs in a dynamic, interactive environment, testing their ability to adapt to feedback. The results suggest that few-shot prompting, especially with semantically relevant examples, improves prediction accuracy. Furthermore, feedback can help LLMs refine their predictions, indicating adaptability in modeling subjective human evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Problem Formulation:** The paper addresses a relevant and potentially impactful problem – predicting human misery scores from text. This continuous regression approach offers a more nuanced understanding of emotional intensity compared to traditional sentiment analysis, setting it apart.
    *   **Comprehensive Prompting Evaluation:** The study rigorously evaluates various prompting strategies, including zero-shot, few-shot, and retrieval-augmented prompting, providing a good comparative analysis. The few-shot approach shows a good improvement in predicting the misery index.
    *   **Innovative Evaluation Framework:** The introduction of the "Misery Game Show" is a significant strength. It moves beyond static evaluation to assess LLMs in a dynamic, interactive setting, probing their adaptive reasoning capabilities through feedback.
    *   **Clear Presentation and Empirical Validation:** The paper is well-structured, clearly presents its methodology, and provides empirical results to support its claims.
    *   **Timeliness:** The research is highly relevant given the increasing interest in affective computing, ethical use of language, and the capabilities/limitations of Large Language models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The dataset consists of only 516 examples. While valuable, this limits the generalizability of the findings. A larger, more diverse dataset would strengthen the study's conclusions.
    *   **Model Scope:** The study primarily focuses on GPT models, hindering a broader understanding of different LLM architectures and their performance on the misery prediction task. Investigating open-source models would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Statistical Analysis:** The paper acknowledges the lack of significance testing. While trends are observed, statistical significance would add rigor and confidence to the reported findings.
    *   **Task Complexity:** The “Misery Game Show” and the task of assigning a misery score is inherently subjective. Although this mirrors real-world scenarios, it introduces a degree of ambiguity and makes the task inherently difficult to evaluate objectively. The results from the "Game Show" simulation, although interesting, are difficult to generalize.
    *   **Ethical Implications:** Although the paper touches on the ethical implications, it could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the potential risks and biases associated with using LLMs to predict human emotion.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
     The paper presents a new approach with the "Misery Game Show" to evaluate LLM adaptability in an emotional reasoning task. By introducing interactive feedback mechanisms, it is one of the first papers to present such a framework. Although there are limitations to the evaluation framework, it presents a new evaluation paradigm.
     The use of LLMs for predicting misery score could potentially be used for sensitive applications like crisis response and mental health support.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** for this paper. While it possesses significant strengths, including a novel evaluation framework and interesting results, the limitations in dataset size, model scope, statistical rigor, and an oversimplified evaluation function prevents it from reaching a higher score. The paper successfully highlights the potential of LLMs in this domain and offers a valuable contribution to the ongoing exploration of affective computing. It introduces a new angle of evaluating LLMs and can potentially lead to further research into emotional reasoning models. However, further work is required to address the limitations and further validate the conclusions.

### GridCodex: A RAG-Driven AI Framework for Power Grid Code Reasoning and Compliance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12682v1)
- **Authors**: Jinquan Shi, Yingying Cheng, Fan Zhang, Miao Jiang, Jun Lin, Yanbai Shen
- **Abstract**: The global shift towards renewable energy presents unprecedented challenges for the electricity industry, making regulatory reasoning and compliance increasingly vital. Grid codes, the regulations governing grid operations, are complex and often lack automated interpretation solutions, which hinders industry expansion and undermines profitability for electricity companies. We introduce GridCodex, an end to end framework for grid code reasoning and compliance that leverages large language models and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). Our framework advances conventional RAG workflows through multi stage query refinement and enhanced retrieval with RAPTOR. We validate the effectiveness of GridCodex with comprehensive benchmarks, including automated answer assessment across multiple dimensions and regulatory agencies. Experimental results showcase a 26.4% improvement in answer quality and more than a 10 fold increase in recall rate. An ablation study further examines the impact of base model selection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GridCodex, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)-driven AI framework designed for automated reasoning and compliance verification of power grid codes. It addresses the challenges of interpreting complex, region-specific grid regulations that typically require extensive human expertise. GridCodex uses multi-stage query refinement and enhanced retrieval with RAPTOR (Recursive Abstraction Processing for Tree-Organized Retrieval) to improve the accuracy and reliability of the RAG process. The framework integrates domain-specific knowledge bases, including terminology definitions and regulatory clauses. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of GridCodex through comprehensive benchmarks and ablation studies, showing improvements over baseline LLM and vanilla RAG approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Real-World Problem:** The paper tackles a significant and practical challenge in the energy industry: the difficulty of interpreting and complying with complex power grid codes. Automation in this area has the potential to reduce operational costs, speed up project timelines, and mitigate compliance risks.
    *   **Novel Application of RAG:**  The paper presents a well-structured and customized RAG pipeline tailored to the nuances of power grid code interpretation. The multi-stage query refinement and use of RAPTOR for enhanced retrieval are noteworthy improvements over basic RAG implementations.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, with benchmarks across multiple regulatory agencies and metrics, including answer quality, faithfulness, and recall. The use of LLM-based evaluators and expert-provided reference answers adds rigor to the evaluation process. The ablation studies provide useful insights into the impact of model size and reasoning capabilities.
    *   **Clear Architecture:** The paper provides a clear description of the GridCodex architecture, breaking it down into the Industry Knowledge, RAG pipeline, and Q&A pipeline components. This clarity facilitates understanding and potential replication.
    *   **Strong Performance Gains:** The experimental results show substantial improvements in answer quality and recall compared to baseline LLM and vanilla RAG methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Proprietary Dataset:** The use of a proprietary HUAWEI grid code dataset limits the reproducibility and generalizability of the results. While the authors state that the dataset contains a diverse set of regulations, it would be beneficial to validate the framework on publicly available grid code datasets.
    *   **Dependency on Open-Source LLMs:** While open-source LLMs are beneficial for accessibility, the long-term viability and performance of GridCodex depend on the continued development and availability of high-quality open-source models.
    *   **Limited Comparison to Existing Domain-Specific Systems:** The paper does a good job contrasting RAG with finetuning, but it would benefit from a more in-depth comparison to any existing domain-specific AI systems used (or proposed) for grid code compliance, even if such systems are based on older technologies. This would clarify the advances presented by GridCodex.
    *   **Limited Insight into Failure Modes:** While the performance metrics are presented well, the paper offers only high-level discussion regarding failure modes (speculative connections with large models or difficulty in understanding ambiguity). A detailed error analysis, including concrete examples of where the GridCodex pipeline struggles, would be valuable.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The novelty lies in the specific application of a RAG framework, enhanced with multi-stage query refinement and RAPTOR, to the domain of power grid code reasoning. While RAG is a known technique, the tailoring to this specific industrial challenge, the integration of domain knowledge, and the significant performance gains demonstrated in the experiments contribute to its significance. The use of hierarchical summarization via RAPTOR in conjunction with a multi-stage refinement pipeline to address complex compliance text is relatively novel. The paper is practically relevant and helps automate a process that would otherwise be time-consuming.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its weaknesses, GridCodex presents a significant contribution to the intersection of AI and the energy industry. The problem it addresses is important, and the RAG-driven solution demonstrates promising results. The authors provide a comprehensive evaluation and insightful ablation studies. However, the use of a proprietary dataset and dependency on open-source LLMs temper the impact and generalizability. The relative lack of direct comparison to existing domain-specific automated systems (if any exist) is an additional factor. Taking these factors into account, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Taxonomy of Hierarchical Multi-Agent Systems: Design Patterns, Coordination Mechanisms, and Industrial Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12683v1)
- **Authors**: David J. Moore
- **Abstract**: Hierarchical multi-agent systems (HMAS) organize collections of agents into layered structures that help manage complexity and scale. These hierarchies can simplify coordination, but they also can introduce trade-offs that are not always obvious. This paper proposes a multi-dimensional taxonomy for HMAS along five axes: control hierarchy, information flow, role and task delegation, temporal layering, and communication structure. The intent is not to prescribe a single "best" design but to provide a lens for comparing different approaches. Rather than treating these dimensions in isolation, the taxonomy is connected to concrete coordination mechanisms - from the long-standing contract-net protocol for task allocation to more recent work in hierarchical reinforcement learning. Industrial contexts illustrate the framework, including power grids and oilfield operations, where agents at production, maintenance, and supply levels coordinate to diagnose well issues or balance energy demand. These cases suggest that hierarchical structures may achieve global efficiency while preserving local autonomy, though the balance is delicate. The paper closes by identifying open challenges: making hierarchical decisions explainable to human operators, scaling to very large agent populations, and assessing whether learning-based agents such as large language models can be safely integrated into layered frameworks. This paper presents what appears to be the first taxonomy that unifies structural, temporal, and communication dimensions of hierarchical MAS into a single design framework, bridging classical coordination mechanisms with modern reinforcement learning and large language model agents.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "A Taxonomy of Hierarchical Multi-Agent Systems: Design Patterns, Coordination Mechanisms, and Industrial Applications." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a multi-dimensional taxonomy for Hierarchical Multi-Agent Systems (HMAS). It categorizes HMAS along five axes: Control Hierarchy, Information Flow, Role and Task Delegation, Temporal Layering, and Communication Structure. The taxonomy aims to provide a framework for comparing different HMAS designs, connecting these dimensions to concrete coordination mechanisms (like the Contract Net Protocol and hierarchical reinforcement learning) and real-world industrial applications (smart grids, oilfield operations).  The paper concludes by identifying open challenges related to explainability, scalability, and integrating learning agents (including LLMs) into hierarchical frameworks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Framework:** The taxonomy is a significant contribution. It integrates structural, temporal, and communicational aspects of HMAS into a single, well-defined framework. This provides a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners in the field.
*   **Clear Definitions:** Each dimension of the taxonomy is clearly defined, with well-articulated extremes, illustrative examples, and a discussion of associated trade-offs.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper effectively bridges the gap between theoretical concepts and practical applications by grounding the taxonomy in real-world industrial contexts (smart grids, oil and gas). The connection to these domains enhances the paper's relevance and usefulness.
*   **Coordination Mechanism Integration:** Linking the taxonomy to specific coordination mechanisms strengthens the framework by demonstrating how design choices along the axes influence the selection and effectiveness of coordination algorithms.
*   **Addresses Important Challenges:** The identification of open challenges (explainability, scalability, integration of learning agents, and security concerns) is timely and relevant, highlighting crucial directions for future research.
*   **Excellent Breadth:** Connects existing coordination mechanisms to modern research in LLMs and RL agents demonstrating excellent breath.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Formal Validation:** The paper would be strengthened by some formal or empirical validation of the taxonomy's usefulness. It mainly relies on illustrative examples and case studies. Some analysis of the literature using the taxonomy could have provided this validation.
*   **Limited Depth in Application Analysis:** While the paper mentions several industrial applications, it could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the design choices and performance implications in these specific contexts. In the oil and gas segment, more specific examples with quantitative results would have boosted the application of the work.
*   **Overlapping Dimensions:** While the authors acknowledge that some dimensions are not fully independent (e.g., control hierarchy and information flow), the overlap could be further addressed. A clearer delineation of the boundaries between these dimensions would enhance the framework's clarity.
*   **LLM Integration is Speculative:** The discussion of LLM integration, while relevant, remains largely speculative due to the emerging nature of the field. It's more of a forward-looking perspective than a fully grounded analysis.
*   **Presentation:** While detailed and descriptive, the layout could be re-arranged for easier digestibility. The taxonomy is the core component of the paper and should be given greater visual prominance.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its integrated taxonomy that combines structural, temporal, and communication dimensions of HMAS. While individual aspects of HMAS have been explored in previous research, the unified framework is a novel contribution. The paper provides valuable organization to a scattered field. The connections to coordination mechanisms and industrial applications, along with the identification of open challenges, enhance its significance.

The taxonomy is a useful lens for analyzing existing systems and could potentially guide the design of new HMAS architectures in the future. It could inform the selection of appropriate coordination mechanisms and identify critical trade-offs in different design choices. It can be used as a scaffolding to generate new insights and understandings for HMAS architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and comprehensive taxonomy that integrates multiple dimensions of HMAS design. While it lacks formal validation and could benefit from more in-depth application analysis, its clear definitions, practical relevance, and timely identification of open challenges make it a significant contribution to the field. The paper offers a strong contribution in design guidance of a complex area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.MA
- **Score**: 7/10

### ToolACE-MT: Non-Autoregressive Generation for Agentic Multi-Turn Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12685v1)
- **Authors**: Xingshan Zeng, Weiwen Liu, Lingzhi Wang, Liangyou Li, Fei Mi, Yasheng Wang, Lifeng Shang, Xin Jiang, Qun Liu
- **Abstract**: Agentic task-solving with Large Language Models (LLMs) requires multi-turn, multi-step interactions, often involving complex function calls and dynamic user-agent exchanges. Existing simulation-based data generation methods for such scenarios rely heavily on costly autoregressive interactions between multiple LLM agents, thereby limiting real-world performance of agentic tasks. In this paper, we propose a novel Non-Autoregressive Iterative Generation framework, called ToolACE-MT, for constructing high-quality multi-turn agentic dialogues. ToolACE-MT generates full conversational trajectories through three stages: coarse-grained initialization, iterative refinement, and offline verification. The initialization phase builds a structurally complete yet semantically coarse dialogue skeleton; the iterative refinement phase introduces realistic complexities and continued refinement via mask-and-fill operations; and the offline verification phase ensures correctness and coherence via rule- and model-based checks. Experiments demonstrate that ToolACE-MT enables efficient, effective and generalizable agentic data generation, offering a new paradigm for high-quality data construction in tool-augmented LLM scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ToolACE-MT: Non-Autoregressive Generation for Agentic Multi-Turn Interaction":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ToolACE-MT, a novel framework for generating high-quality multi-turn agentic dialogues where agents interact with users and tools to solve tasks. The key innovation is its non-autoregressive (NAT) approach, addressing the limitations of existing simulation-based methods that rely on costly autoregressive interactions between LLM agents. ToolACE-MT operates in three stages: 1) **Coarse-Grained Initialization**, which creates a skeletal dialogue structure, 2) **Iterative Refinement**, progressively enriching the skeleton with complexities using mask-and-fill techniques, and 3) **Offline Verification**, ensuring correctness and coherence through rule- and model-based checks. Experimental results on various agentic benchmarks (BFCL, T-Bench, ACEBench) demonstrate that models trained with ToolACE-MT generated data outperform those trained with autoregressive multi-agent simulation data in terms of efficiency, complexity control, and data quality. The paper also explores the benefits of iterative refinement and verification through ablation studies, as well as the generalizability of the framework with respect to model backbones.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of non-autoregressive generation techniques to the domain of multi-turn agentic dialogue data creation. Existing methods rely on autoregressive interactions, which are computationally expensive and lack global contextual awareness. Adapting NAT principles allows for parallel generation and iterative refinement, offering significant efficiency gains.

However, the individual components (mask-and-fill, rule-based verification) are not entirely new. The paper's contribution is in *how* these known techniques are combined and adapted for a new and pressing problem. The key contribution is a more efficient and controllable methodology for generating agentic interaction data.

**Significance:** High-quality training data is crucial for developing capable agentic LLMs. The cost and complexity of current data generation approaches are major bottlenecks. ToolACE-MT addresses this bottleneck, making it easier and more efficient to produce the data required for training these models. The ability to explicitly control task complexity and ensure consistency are significant advantages.

The benchmarks used (BFCL, T-Bench, ACEBench) are well-established in the agentic LLM research community. By demonstrating substantial performance gains over existing data generation techniques across a range of benchmarks, the authors strengthen the claims regarding the effectiveness of ToolACE-MT.

The ablation studies convincingly demonstrate the importance of each stage (Initialization, Refinement, Verification) for achieving optimal performance. The framework is readily adaptable to different LLM backbones, enhancing its usability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   While the paper claims better complexity control, specific metrics or analysis detailing *how* complexity is controlled more precisely would be beneficial.
*   The dependence on a "good" LLM for generating/refining content remains. The quality of the data generated by ToolACE-MT is bottlenecked by the models leveraged in initialization, iterative refinement, and verification stages. This dependency should be better acknowledged.
*   Although the cost analysis is provided, a more thorough breakdown of the costs associated with each stage of the ToolACE-MT pipeline would be appreciated.
*   The discussion regarding potential biases introduced by iterative refinement and verification is limited. Since models are used, bias inherent in those models may seep into the data generation process.
*   While the framework improves efficiency, the process of initially building the tool pool, defining task structures, and designing prompts is still somewhat manual and time-consuming.

**Potential Influence:**

ToolACE-MT has the potential to significantly influence how agentic LLMs are trained. Its efficiency and control mechanisms could accelerate the development and deployment of more capable and reliable agentic systems. It also opens up avenues for more targeted data generation strategies, focusing on specific areas where models struggle. The adoption of this framework by other researchers could lead to more readily available datasets for tool-augmented LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated framework for generating multi-turn agentic dialogue data. The non-autoregressive approach is a valuable contribution, addressing a key bottleneck in the development of agentic LLMs. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods. The ablation studies clearly highlight the importance of each component. However, the dependency on pre-existing LLMs, the reliance on human expertise for initial tooling, and lack of detail on complexity control metrics temper the overall impact.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EGOILLUSION: Benchmarking Hallucinations in Egocentric Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12687v1)
- **Authors**: Ashish Seth, Utkarsh Tyagi, Ramaneswaran Selvakumar, Nishit Anand, Sonal Kumar, Sreyan Ghosh, Ramani Duraiswami, Chirag Agarwal, Dinesh Manocha
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in complex multimodal tasks. While MLLMs excel at visual perception and reasoning in third-person and egocentric videos, they are prone to hallucinations, generating coherent yet inaccurate responses. We present EgoIllusion, a first benchmark to evaluate MLLM hallucinations in egocentric videos. EgoIllusion comprises 1,400 videos paired with 8,000 human-annotated open and closed-ended questions designed to trigger hallucinations in both visual and auditory cues in egocentric videos. Evaluations across ten MLLMs reveal significant challenges, including powerful models like GPT-4o and Gemini, achieving only 59% accuracy. EgoIllusion lays the foundation in developing robust benchmarks to evaluate the effectiveness of MLLMs and spurs the development of better egocentric MLLMs with reduced hallucination rates. Our benchmark will be open-sourced for reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EGOILLUSION, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate hallucinations in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) specifically when processing egocentric videos. The benchmark consists of 1,400 videos with 8,000 human-annotated question-answer pairs, targeting visual and auditory cues. The questions are designed to elicit hallucinations related to actions, objects, sounds, and temporal order.  The paper evaluates ten MLLMs and finds that even powerful models like GPT-4o and Gemini struggle, achieving only 59% accuracy.  The authors analyze model responses to identify the sources of hallucinations and discuss the challenges MLLMs face in processing egocentric video.  The benchmark is intended to be open-sourced.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength is the introduction of a dedicated benchmark for *egocentric* video hallucination in MLLMs.  While hallucination benchmarks exist for images and third-person videos, EGOILLUSION addresses the unique challenges posed by the first-person perspective, including dynamic object states, occlusions, and rich multi-sensory information. This focus on egocentric video is a significant contribution. The hallucination strategies (prompt injection, adversarial sampling, manipulating temporal order) build upon existing techniques but adapt them to the egocentric domain.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark's significance lies in highlighting the vulnerability of current MLLMs to hallucinations in a practically important domain (egocentric video, which is relevant for robotics, AR/VR, and assistive technologies). The paper demonstrates that even state-of-the-art models struggle to achieve high accuracy. The detailed analysis of the sources of hallucinations (actions, objects, sounds, temporal understanding) is valuable for guiding future research. The analysis about performance differences between reasoning and perception tasks are also relevant. The observation that smaller models can be competitive with larger ones on this benchmark is also interesting and calls into question the over-reliance on scaling up for these kinds of complex multi-modal tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:** The benchmark relies on human annotation, which can be expensive and subject to bias. The paper reports inter-annotator agreement, mitigating this concern, but a more diverse set of annotators could further improve the robustness of the benchmark. The results show a relatively small gap between open and closed-source models, and more analysis on why that is the case (related to the data, the models, or the specific evaluation methodology) would make the paper stronger.  The paper could include more details about video selection (e.g. inclusion/exclusion criteria)

*   **Impact:** EGOILLUSION has the potential to spur the development of more robust MLLMs specifically tailored for egocentric video understanding. The benchmark provides a valuable tool for researchers to evaluate and compare different approaches to hallucination mitigation. The open-sourcing of the benchmark is a positive step that will facilitate further research in this area.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The score is justified because EGOILLUSION fills a real gap in the evaluation of MLLMs, providing a needed resource for progress in egocentric understanding. The techniques are generally sound. While the paper has some weaknesses in terms of human annotation biases and limited diversity of the tested models, these shortcomings do not undermine the contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Deadline-Aware Bandwidth Allocation for Semantic Generative Communication with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12701v1)
- **Authors**: Jinhyuk Choi, Jihong Park, Seungeun Oh, Seong-Lyun Kim
- **Abstract**: The importance of Radio Access Network (RAN) in support Artificial Intelligence (AI) application services has grown significantly, underscoring the need for an integrated approach that considers not only network efficiency but also AI performance. In this paper we focus on a semantic generative communication (SGC) framework for image inpainting application. Specifically, the transmitter sends semantic information, i.e., semantic masks and textual descriptions, while the receiver utilizes a conditional diffusion model on a base image, using them as conditioning data to produce the intended image. In this framework, we propose a bandwidth allocation scheme designed to maximize bandwidth efficiency while ensuring generation performance. This approach is based on our finding of a Semantic Deadline--the minimum time that conditioning data is required to be injected to meet a given performance threshold--within the multi-modal SGC framework. Given this observation, the proposed scheme allocates limited bandwidth so that each semantic information can be transmitted within the corresponding semantic deadline. Experimental results corroborate that the proposed bandwidth allocation scheme achieves higher generation performance in terms of PSNR for a given bandwidth compared to traditional schemes that do not account for semantic deadlines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of efficient bandwidth allocation in semantic generative communication (SGC) systems, specifically for image inpainting applications using diffusion models.  In SGC, instead of transmitting the image directly, the transmitter sends semantic information (masks and text descriptions) that the receiver uses to generate the intended image using a conditional diffusion model. The core contribution is a novel bandwidth allocation scheme that leverages the concept of a "Semantic Deadline." The authors observed that each piece of semantic information has a minimum time within which it must be delivered to maximize generation performance. The proposed scheme allocates bandwidth to ensure that each semantic input is transmitted within its semantic deadline, leading to improved inpainting quality (PSNR) for a given bandwidth compared to traditional schemes. The paper includes experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed scheme.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the introduction of the "Semantic Deadline" concept and its application to bandwidth allocation in SGC.  While the individual components (diffusion models, semantic communication, bandwidth allocation) are not new, the combination and the novel application of semantic deadlines offer a distinct contribution.  The key idea is tailoring bandwidth allocation to the characteristics of diffusion models, specifically how different semantic inputs affect the generation process over time. This is a non-trivial insight.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the efficiency of AI-powered communication systems. With the rise of AIGC services, efficient resource utilization in wireless networks is becoming increasingly important.  By considering the specific requirements of generative AI models, the proposed approach can optimize bandwidth allocation for better inpainting quality. The experimental results demonstrating performance improvements (PSNR) validate the practical relevance. It potentially opens avenues for more efficient and personalized resource allocation in AIGC-driven communication scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of bandwidth allocation for semantic generative image inpainting.
    *   **Novel concept:** The "Semantic Deadline" provides a practical mechanism to characterize semantic inputs based on time sensitivity.
    *   **Heuristic optimization approach:** the allocation algorithm effectively addresses the complex relationship between transmission and computation domains by using deadline information.
    *   **Experimental validation:** The experimental results demonstrate performance improvement compared to baseline algorithms.
    *   **Practical relevance:** The application of the proposed solution to image inpainting is timely and relevant to emerging AIGC services.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to image inpainting using Stable Diffusion and specific data sets (CelebA-HQ).  It remains to be seen how well the concept generalizes to other generative models and different application scenarios.
    *   **Heuristic Approach:**  While the approach is effective, there is limited discussion about the optimality of the solution.  The heuristic approach provides efficient allocation, but the tightness of bounds is unknown.
    *   **Channel model simplification:** Rayleigh fading is a common channel model, but the assumption of perfect channel equalization might be an oversimplification in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** Benchmark algorithms are limited in that they don't fully exploit the semantic characteristics of generative AI.  A comparison with more AI aware approaches could strengthen the result.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in resource allocation for AI-powered communication. The "Semantic Deadline" concept could be extended to other AI tasks and communication modalities.  Furthermore, the paper highlights the importance of considering AI model characteristics when designing communication systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 is appropriate.  The introduction of the "Semantic Deadline" concept and its experimental validation demonstrate a clear contribution to the field. However, the limited scope of the experiments, the use of a heuristic approach, and some simplifying assumptions limit the overall impact. The paper is not revolutionary, but provides a solid advance in the field, which justifies the assigned score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: eess.SY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Asymmetric Diffusion Recommendation Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12706v1)
- **Authors**: Yongchun Zhu, Guanyu Jiang, Jingwu Chen, Feng Zhang, Xiao Yang, Zuotao Liu
- **Abstract**: Recently, motivated by the outstanding achievements of diffusion models, the diffusion process has been employed to strengthen representation learning in recommendation systems. Most diffusion-based recommendation models typically utilize standard Gaussian noise in symmetric forward and reverse processes in continuous data space. Nevertheless, the samples derived from recommendation systems inhabit a discrete data space, which is fundamentally different from the continuous one. Moreover, Gaussian noise has the potential to corrupt personalized information within latent representations. In this work, we propose a novel and effective method, named Asymmetric Diffusion Recommendation Model (AsymDiffRec), which learns forward and reverse processes in an asymmetric manner. We define a generalized forward process that simulates the missing features in real-world recommendation samples. The reverse process is then performed in an asymmetric latent feature space. To preserve personalized information within the latent representation, a task-oriented optimization strategy is introduced. In the serving stage, the raw sample with missing features is regarded as a noisy input to generate a denoising and robust representation for the final prediction. By equipping base models with AsymDiffRec, we conduct online A/B tests, achieving improvements of +0.131% and +0.166% in terms of users' active days and app usage duration respectively. Additionally, the extended offline experiments also demonstrate improvements. AsymDiffRec has been implemented in the Douyin Music App.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Asymmetric Diffusion Recommendation Model":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel diffusion-based recommendation model, AsymDiffRec, designed to address limitations of existing diffusion-based approaches in recommendation systems.  Specifically, it targets two issues:  the inappropriateness of Gaussian noise in discrete data spaces inherent to recommendation and the potential corruption of personalized information during the standard symmetric diffusion process.  AsymDiffRec introduces an asymmetric forward and reverse process. The forward process simulates missing features via feature dropout (a discrete operation), making the 'noisy' data more realistic. The reverse process operates in the latent feature space (as opposed to the raw data space) to directly reconstruct latent representations, preserving personalized information. A task-oriented optimization strategy (auxiliary loss) further helps retain personalized information. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of AsymDiffRec through online A/B testing on the Douyin Music App, reporting improvements in user activity and engagement. They also present offline experiments that demonstrate superiority compared to other baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:

    *   **Asymmetric Diffusion:** The core idea of an asymmetric diffusion process, with feature dropout in the raw feature space for the forward process and latent space reconstruction for the reverse process, is a significant departure from standard diffusion-based recommendation models. This asymmetry is specifically designed to better model discrete data and preserve personalization.
    *   **Feature Dropout as Noise:**  Using feature dropout to simulate noise in the forward process is a more realistic approach for recommendation systems compared to adding Gaussian noise. This resonates with the practical problem of missing user or item features in real-world recommendation scenarios.
    *   **Task-Oriented Optimization:**  The use of an auxiliary loss function alongside the reconstruction loss to guide the learning of personalized information is a clever approach that enhances model performance, particularly compared to directly reconstructing the input.

*   **Significance:** The paper has several potentially significant contributions:

    *   **Addressing a Practical Problem:** It tackles the issue of missing features, a common challenge in real-world recommender systems. The feature dropout mechanism can directly address the noise present in the data collected.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The A/B testing results on the Douyin Music App are compelling and demonstrate that the model provides real-world improvements in key metrics (user activity and engagement).  This industrial deployment strengthens the paper's significance.
    *   **Improved Representation Learning:**  The architecture directly helps learn a more robust and personalized representation from sparse or noisy real world data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear Problem Definition: The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing diffusion models for recommendation.
    *   Well-Motivated Approach: The proposed approach is logically motivated and justified based on the specific challenges in recommendation systems.
    *   Strong Empirical Results: The paper provides thorough experimental results, including both offline and online evaluations, to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   Real-World Deployment: The successful deployment of AsymDiffRec in a large-scale music recommendation app is a testament to its practical value.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Lack of Public Dataset Evaluation: The lack of evaluation on public datasets is a drawback.  While the authors justify this by stating that existing datasets don't have enough features for their feature dropout mechanism, it makes it difficult to directly compare AsymDiffRec with other diffusion-based models published using public datasets.
    *   Limited Baseline Comparison: While the baselines used (CDAE, MultiVAE, SSL, and DiffRec) are valid, exploring more state-of-the-art (SOTA) representation learning techniques or other diffusion-based models might have strengthened the comparison. Also, what is considered as *the base model* in Table 1 has not been defined appropriately.
    *   Details of the Denoising Function: The description of the denoising function (a two-layer neural network) is rather brief, and more information about its architecture and training process would be beneficial.
    *   Ablation study contains gaps: The ablation study in Table 2 is good for showing that reconstruction loss and auxiliary task-oriented loss are both needed, but one of the entries "AsymDiffRec w/o aux" performs worse than base, which requires more investigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in diffusion-based recommendation models by highlighting the importance of considering the discrete nature of recommendation data and the need to preserve personalized information. The real-world deployment in Douyin Music App also provides a strong proof of concept and encourages further exploration of this approach.
    It would also be useful to investigate how this approach works when combined with other model explainability frameworks.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach (asymmetric diffusion, feature dropout as noise, and task-oriented learning) for recommendation systems, with strong empirical evidence from real-world A/B testing in Douyin Music App. This suggests that the improvements are not just performance gains in a lab setting, but a change in behavior and data collected from real users. However, the lack of evaluation on public datasets, limited baseline comparison, and the brief description of the denoising function slightly limit the paper's overall impact and reproducibility. The novelty is substantial, and the industrial validation significantly increases its value, justifying a score above average.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Single-Reference Text-to-Image Manipulation with Dual Contrastive Denoising Score
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12718v1)
- **Authors**: Syed Muhmmad Israr, Feng Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large-scale text-to-image generative models have shown remarkable ability to synthesize diverse and high-quality images. However, it is still challenging to directly apply these models for editing real images for two reasons. First, it is difficult for users to come up with a perfect text prompt that accurately describes every visual detail in the input image. Second, while existing models can introduce desirable changes in certain regions, they often dramatically alter the input content and introduce unexpected changes in unwanted regions. To address these challenges, we present Dual Contrastive Denoising Score, a simple yet powerful framework that leverages the rich generative prior of text-to-image diffusion models. Inspired by contrastive learning approaches for unpaired image-to-image translation, we introduce a straightforward dual contrastive loss within the proposed framework. Our approach utilizes the extensive spatial information from the intermediate representations of the self-attention layers in latent diffusion models without depending on auxiliary networks. Our method achieves both flexible content modification and structure preservation between input and output images, as well as zero-shot image-to-image translation. Through extensive experiments, we show that our approach outperforms existing methods in real image editing while maintaining the capability to directly utilize pretrained text-to-image diffusion models without further training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Dual Contrastive Denoising Score" (DualCDS), a novel framework for text-guided image manipulation using pre-trained text-to-image diffusion models.  DualCDS aims to address the challenges of editing real images by preserving the structural elements of the original image while adapting it to a new textual description.  The core idea is to leverage self-attention layers within Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs) to capture structural information and combine this with a dual contrastive loss inspired by unpaired image-to-image translation. The method uses delta denoising score (DDS) to transfer style features from target text and spatial and shape features of the source images are preserved using dual contrastive loss. The approach is training-free and achieves zero-shot image-to-image translation. The authors demonstrate through experiments that DualCDS outperforms existing methods in real image editing, achieving a better balance between content transformation and structure preservation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of existing techniques and the way they are applied to the image editing problem. The idea of using self-attention layers for structure preservation isn't entirely new, and contrastive learning has been used in image translation before. The unique aspect is the integration of dual contrastive loss, delta denoising score, and attention layers representation within the latent diffusion model for text guided image manipulation. This integration enables zero-shot image manipulation without requiring additional networks. This specific combination and its effectiveness represent a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its ability to address a key challenge in text-guided image editing: maintaining structural consistency while allowing for flexible content modification. The training-free nature of the approach is also a significant advantage, making it more accessible and practical. A critical advantage is that the method works effectively without requiring any extra networks or fine-tuning/training. Furthermore, the performance comparisons with current methods such as SDEdit, Prompt-to-Prompt Editing, PnP, CDS, and Mutual Self-attention Control (MasaCtrl) highlights its practical significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Effective Combination of Techniques:** The integration of dual contrastive learning with DDS and attention layers demonstrates strong practical results.
    *   **Training-Free Approach:**  The method avoids the need for task-specific training, making it readily applicable to a wide range of editing tasks.
    *   **Preservation of Structural Details:** A key strength is the ability to retain fine-grained structural features while modifying the image based on text prompts.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes both qualitative and quantitative evaluations, as well as user studies, to support its claims.
    *   **Clear motivation and problem definition:** the introduction clearly defines the shortcomings of current text guided image editing technologies and precisely introduce the proposed methodology and solutions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The method relies on the quality and capabilities of the underlying pre-trained diffusion model. It inherits the biases and limitations inherent in such models.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance is likely sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters, such as the weight of the contrastive loss and patch size, requiring careful tuning for different tasks. The analysis on different values of lambda (λ) and patch sizes partially addresses this, but optimal settings could still vary significantly depending on the images and prompts.

* **Potential Influence:**

    *   DualCDS has the potential to become a valuable tool for content creators and researchers working on image editing and generation. Its training-free nature and ability to produce high-quality results could make it a popular choice for various applications. The insights into the use of self-attention and contrastive learning could also inspire further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 is appropriate. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by presenting an effective and practical approach to text-guided image editing. While the individual components aren't entirely groundbreaking, the specific combination and the resulting performance justify a positive evaluation. Some weaknesses include sensitivity to hyperparameters and dependence on pre-trained models, which detract from the overall score. It also introduces an entirely training free method for text guided image editing which is a significant milestone. Further the experiments also validate the performance improvements.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GTool: Graph Enhanced Tool Planning with Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12725v1)
- **Authors**: Wenjie Chen, Wenbin Li, Di Yao, Xuying Meng, Chang Gong, Jingping Bi
- **Abstract**: Tool planning with large language models (LLMs), referring to selecting, organizing, and preparing the tools necessary to complete a user request, bridges the gap between natural language understanding and task execution. However, current works treat different tools as isolated components and fail to leverage the inherent dependencies of tools, leading to invalid planning results. Since tool dependencies are often incomplete, it becomes challenging for LLMs to accurately identify the appropriate tools required by a user request, especially when confronted with a large toolset. To solve this challenge, we propose \texttt{GTool}, which is the first work aiming to enhance the tool planning ability of LLMs under incomplete dependencies. \texttt{GTool} constructs a request-specific tool graph to select tools efficiently and generate the \texttt{<graph token>} which provides sufficient dependency information understandable by LLMs. Moreover, a missing dependency prediction task is designed to improve the reliability of \texttt{GTool} with incomplete dependencies. Without trimming LLMs, \texttt{GTool} can be seamlessly integrated with various LLM backbones without extensive retraining. Extensive experiments show that \texttt{GTool} achieves more than 29.6\% performance improvements compared with the state-of-the-art (SOTA) baselines with a light-weight (7B) LLM backbone.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GTool, a novel approach to enhance tool planning with Large Language Models (LLMs) by explicitly modeling tool dependencies. Unlike existing methods that treat tools as isolated components, GTool constructs a request-specific tool graph, leveraging tool descriptions and dependencies, and introduces a missing dependency prediction task. A GNN-based module is trained to generate a  <graph token> which is injected into the LLM's prompt, providing crucial structural information.  The approach is tuning-free for the LLM backbone, requiring only training the GNN, and is evaluated across multiple datasets and LLMs. The experiments demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to state-of-the-art baselines, especially in scenarios with incomplete tool dependencies and large toolsets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in explicitly addressing the issue of incomplete tool dependencies in LLM-based tool planning. Constructing a request-specific tool graph and training a GNN to generate graph embeddings is a valuable contribution. The introduction of a missing dependency prediction task is also a novel method for improving robustness. The work's claim to be the first attempt to enhance tool planning under incomplete dependencies is credible given the prior art.

**Significance:** Tool planning is a crucial aspect of enabling LLMs to solve complex tasks.  The problem of incomplete dependencies is a real-world challenge that significantly impacts the reliability of LLM-based agents. By addressing this, GTool contributes to making tool planning more practical and robust. The demonstrated performance improvements are significant, showing the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The fact that GTool is tuning-free for the LLM is also important, as it allows for easy integration with various LLM backbones. The efficiency gains reported, particularly in token consumption and inference time, are also relevant to real-world deployment. The scalability experiments on ToolBench showcase the method's potential for use in large-scale API ecosystems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses an important limitation:** Tackles the practical problem of incomplete tool dependencies.
*   **Novel Approach:** Combines graph-based methods with LLMs in a unique way.
*   **Significant Performance Gains:** Achieves substantial improvements over strong baselines.
*   **Tuning-Free LLM Integration:** Easy to integrate with different LLMs.
*   **Efficient:** Reduces token consumption and inference time.
*   **Scalable:** Demonstrates performance on a large-scale dataset.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Experiments on multiple datasets and LLMs with ablation studies.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **GNN Training:** While the LLM is frozen, the GNN requires training data.  The quality and quantity of this data could impact performance. The paper could benefit from a deeper discussion on how the GNN is trained and the sensitivity to the training dataset.
*   **Simple Prompts:** While minimizing prompt tokens is good for efficiency, the use of a very simple prompt containing only tool names in the LLM module implies that the model relies heavily on the GNN for information. This might limit performance in extremely sparse dependency graphs.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the case studies in section C provide some analysis, it could be further strengthened by analyzing patterns in failure cases more systematically, to identify specific limitations of GTool.

**Potential Influence:** GTool has the potential to influence future research in tool planning by emphasizing the importance of modeling tool dependencies and providing a concrete framework for doing so.  The work could also inspire the development of new methods for learning and representing tool dependencies.  The efficient design makes the work more practical for real-world use cases.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8. The paper tackles a relevant and practical problem within the field of LLM-based tool planning. The approach is novel, well-motivated, and shows significant improvements over existing methods. The tuning-free aspect and efficiency gains are valuable contributions. However, there are some minor limitations, particularly in the analysis of failure cases and the sensitivity to the GNN training data that prevent it from achieving a higher score. The simple prompt architecture implies a strong dependency on the GNN and might limit the method's performance in extreme scenarios, where the tools description and context are necessary for selecting the appropriate tools. The work, though strong, isn't quite groundbreaking enough to warrant a higher score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### DESIGNER: Design-Logic-Guided Multidisciplinary Data Synthesis for LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12726v1)
- **Authors**: Weize Liu, Yongchi Zhao, Yijia Luo, Mingyu Xu, Jiaheng Liu, Yanan Li, Xiguo Hu, Yuchi Xu, Wenbo Su, Bo Zheng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in many natural language tasks but still struggle with complex, multi-step reasoning, particularly across diverse disciplines. Existing reasoning datasets often either lack disciplinary breadth or the structural depth necessary to elicit robust reasoning behaviors. We propose DESIGNER: a DESIGN-logic-guidEd Reasoning data synthesis pipeline that leverages naturally available, extensive raw documents (book corpus and web corpus) to generate multidisciplinary challenging questions. A core innovation of our approach is the introduction of a Design Logic concept, which mimics the question-creation process of human educators. We use LLMs to reverse-engineer and abstract over 120,000 design logics from existing questions across various disciplines. By matching these design logics with disciplinary source materials, we are able to create reasoning questions that far surpass the difficulty and diversity of existing datasets. Based on this pipeline, we synthesized two large-scale reasoning datasets that span 75 disciplines: Design-Logic-Reasoning-Book (DLR-Book), containing 3.04 million challenging questions synthesized from the book corpus, and Design-Logic-Reasoning-Web (DLR-Web), with 1.66 million challenging questions from the web corpus.
